=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N slimes lined up in a row from left to right.
For i = 1, 2, \ldots, N, the i-th slime from the left has size A_i.
For each K = 1, 2, \ldots, N, solve the following problem.

Takahashi is the K-th slime from the left in the initial state.
Find the maximum size that he can have after performing the following action any number of times, possibly zero:

- Choose a slime adjacent to him that is strictly smaller than him, and absorb it.
As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and Takahashi's size increases by the size of the absorbed slime.

When a slime disappears due to absorption, the gap is immediately closed, and the slimes that were adjacent to the disappearing slime (if they exist) become adjacent (see the explanation in Sample Input 1).

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N

Output

Print the answers B_K for each K = 1, 2, \ldots, N, separated by spaces, in the following format:
B_1 B_2 \ldots B_N

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 5 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 10^9
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
4 13 2 3 2 6

Sample Output 1

4 30 2 13 2 13

As an example, consider the problem for K = 4.
We will refer to the sizes of the remaining slimes, listed from left to right, with Takahashi's size enclosed in square brackets [ ], as the state of the row.
That is, the initial state is (4, 13, 2, [3], 2, 6). Consider the following sequence of actions by Takahashi.

- He absorbs the slime to his right. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 3 + 2 = 5. The state becomes (4, 13, 2, [5], 6).
- He absorbs the slime to his left. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 5 + 2 = 7. The state becomes (4, 13, [7], 6).
- He absorbs the slime to his right. As a result, the absorbed slime disappears, and his size becomes 7 + 6 = 13. The state becomes (4, 13, [13]).

There are no slimes adjacent to him that are strictly smaller than him, so he cannot perform any more actions. His final size is 13, which is the maximum possible.

Sample Input 2

12
22 25 61 10 21 37 2 14 5 8 6 24

Sample Output 2

22 47 235 10 31 235 2 235 5 235 6 235

```python

```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Suppose we have an interval [l,r] with sum S. It can absorb left neighbor a if a < S. After absorbing, new sum S+a. If a >= S, cannot absorb it now. To ever absorb it, we must first absorb some elements on the right (if any) to increase S. But if right side is blocked by a boundary b >= current S, maybe need absorb left? There is mutual blocking.

Consider a "valley" between two large elements. Starting inside, can absorb all smaller elements until sum reaches one of the large elements. If sum surpasses a large element, absorb it, gaining its mass, then continue.

This is similar to "flood fill with water level" where sum acts as water level; to cross a barrier of height h, need water level > h. Absorbing a barrier adds its height to water level. Actually if barrier value h < S, crossing increases S by h. If h >= S, cannot cross until S increases from other side. So barriers are elements; to cross a barrier, need current sum > barrier value.

Maybe we can process from small to large using DSU? Since an element of value x can be absorbed by any adjacent component with sum > x. If we maintain components that are "active" (Takahashi intervals) maybe not.

Maybe reachability can be expressed by a "peeling" condition: There exists a boundary element that is < sum of rest, recursively. This is similar to "can reduce interval to K" and maybe equivalent to "there exists a boundary element x with 2x < total sum" and the remaining interval is reducible. Notice condition A_L < sum([L+1,R]) is equivalent to sum([L,R]) > 2 A_L. So if a boundary element is more than half of total sum, it cannot be removed first (nor maybe at all? For interval I with sum S, reverse peeling removes all elements except K. The condition for a removal order is: for each removed element x, x < S - sum(removed before x). Final intervals are laminar (non-crossing): for any two starts, their final intervals are either disjoint or one contains the other. This is a huge structural property.

Maybe we can compute final interval by repeatedly merging with the smaller of the two adjacent elements? Since to absorb a neighbor, it must be < current sum. Maybe we can model the reverse process globally: Starting from a candidate interval I, we can remove boundary elements if they are less than half the current total. Let's focus on reverse process. It might be easier to compute all K by considering intervals and their "center of mass" after peeling. This is correct: C(I) is union of survivors of the two subintervals obtained by removing a removable boundary. Base C([i,i])={i}. This is a recursive definition. This C(I) recursion is elegant. We need for each K the largest closed interval I containing K with K in C(I). Since if I is closed and K in C(I), final = I. The largest such I? Let's analyze C(I) more. The recursion:
C([i,i]) = {i}.
For length >1:
If 2A_L < S, include C([L+1,R]).
If 2A_R < S, include C([L,R-1]).
If neither, C(I)=∅.

This is exactly the set of positions that are "survivors" of the interval under the operation of deleting boundary elements with value < half of current sum. This is reminiscent of the "survivor" in a game where you remove endpoints if they are less than half the total. The condition 2A_L < S depends on total sum S, which is fixed for the interval. So for a fixed interval I, whether a boundary is removable is determined solely by S and boundary values. If removable, the survivors are inherited from the smaller interval. Thus C(I) can be computed if we know C for subintervals. But there are O(N^2) intervals.

However, note C(I) is always a subset of positions that are "not removed" in some process. Could it be that C(I) is nonempty iff there exists a position K such that all elements except K can be removed; this is equivalent to the interval being "K-reachable". We need max closed interval.

Maybe we can compute final intervals by considering all intervals I that are "closed" and C(I) nonempty. The recursion for C(I) only depends on removing boundaries. If I is closed, then outside boundaries are >= S. But for C(I), inside boundaries may be removable.

Could there be a way to compute C(I) for all intervals using a "meet-in-the-middle" with prefix sums? Let's examine the condition for a specific K to be in C(I). In reverse, starting from I, we need to remove all elements except K. The order of removals is constrained by boundaries. But maybe there is a simple characterization in terms of the maximum element on each side.

For K to be in C(I), consider the left part [L, K-1] and right part [K+1, R]. In reverse, elements on the left of K must be removed from the left boundary? Actually to remove an element to the left of K, it must eventually become the left boundary of the remaining interval. Since K is not removed, the left boundary of the remaining interval will be the leftmost remaining element. Elements to the left of K are removed in order from L to K-1 (left to right) because only the leftmost can be removed on that side. Similarly, elements to the right of K are removed in order from R down to K+1 (right to left). Thus the reverse removal order is: some interleaving of removing the current left boundary (starting at L) and current right boundary (starting at R), until only K remains. This is exactly the same as the original forward expansion but reversed. Therefore, for a fixed K, the condition for I to be reachable is: There exists an interleaving of the left sequence (L, L+1, ..., K-1) and right sequence (R, R-1, ..., K+1) such that each removed element x satisfies x < current total - x (i.e., 2x < current total). Current total decreases by removed elements. This is equivalent to forward: starting with A_K, add elements in reverse order (K-1..L and K+1..R) with condition x < current sum.

This is like two sequences. Maybe we can compute the maximal interval for K by expanding left/right using the greedy "smaller boundary" rule. For a fixed K, the greedy algorithm is optimal. Could we compute all K using a divide-and-conquer on the greedy expansion tree?

Let's think about the greedy process in terms of "minimum boundary". At each step, choose the smaller of the two boundary elements (if exists) and absorb it if < current sum. This is equivalent to the following: The order in which elements are absorbed is the order of a priority queue where the key of an element is its value? Actually if we always absorb the smaller boundary, then the sequence of absorbed values is nondecreasing? Let's check. Suppose current interval, left boundary a, right boundary b, assume a<=b. We absorb a if a<S. New sum S+a. The new left boundary c could be less than a, equal, or greater. The next min boundary might be c or b. Could be less than a. Example S=10, left boundary a=5, right b=100. Absorb a=5, new left boundary c=1. Next min c=1 < previous a. So absorbed sequence 5,1 not nondecreasing. But if c=1, why wasn't it boundary before? Because a was between c and K; to reach c, had to absorb a first. So values can decrease. Thus not simple.

But note if a is absorbed, the next boundary on that side c could be smaller than a. That means we crossed a "peak" a to a smaller valley. This is possible because sum increased by a. This is like expanding over mountains.

Maybe we can use a segment tree to find "next element greater than current sum" on each side. For a fixed K, the greedy process can be simulated by jumping over runs of elements that are all < current sum? But after absorbing a run, sum increases, so the stopping element may become absorbable. This is similar to "while the minimum boundary < S, absorb it". The minimum boundary can be found with a data structure.

For all K, maybe we can use the laminar tree and compute it via a "priority queue of intervals" in O(N log N). Let's search memory: There is an AtCoder problem "AGC 001 F? Wide Swap" no. The solution might use "Cartesian tree of A_i" and "prefix sums" with "DSU on tree".

But how to compute C(I) for all intervals efficiently? Maybe C(I) can be characterized by a simple "maximum" condition. Let's study C(I) recursion more.

For interval I, C(I) is the set of positions that can survive. The recursion says C(I) is the union of C of subintervals obtained by removing a boundary if that boundary is "light" (2x < S). This is analogous to the following: Start with the interval I and repeatedly remove all boundary elements that are light; the remaining positions are C(I)? Wait if a boundary is light, we can remove it, but we don't have to. C(I) is the set of possible final survivors, not a single survivor. If we remove all possible light boundaries in any order, the set of elements that can never be removed are the "core". Actually an element K is in C(I) iff there exists a sequence ending at K. This is like the set of possible winners in a deletion game. There is a known concept: In such games, the set of possible winners can be computed by the "maximum" or "minimum" using interval DP, but maybe for this specific rule it is the set of positions that are "not dominated" by adjacent intervals.

Maybe direct positions of an interval I are exactly the positions K in I such that A_K is a "record" in the peeling process, i.e., K can "eat" the whole interval. There can be multiple.

Let's derive a condition for K in C(I) using the two-sided expansion. For a fixed K, to absorb all of I, starting with A_K, we need to expand left and right. This is equivalent to: There exists an order of adding elements from left side (K-1 down to L) and right side (K+1 up to R) such that each added x < current sum. Since we can choose order, the optimal strategy is to always add the smaller available boundary (as argued). Thus K in C(I) iff the greedy algorithm starting at K with target interval I absorbs all elements of I (and doesn't need outside). This can be checked by simulating the two sequences.

Can we characterize K in C(I) in terms of prefix sums? Let's define left sequence values l_1=A_{K-1}, l_2=A_{K-2}, ..., l_p=A_L (from K outward left). Right sequence r_1=A_{K+1}, ..., r_q=A_R. We start with sum a=A_K. At each step, choose the smaller of the next left value and next right value (the current boundary) if it is < current sum. This is exactly the merge of two sequences by increasing value? Actually we choose min of the two current heads. This is like we have two stacks (left sequence and right sequence) and we pop the smaller head if it's less than current sum. This is analogous to merging two sequences into a single sequence sorted by the order of heads, but heads change after popping.

This process is deterministic for given K and interval I. It will absorb all iff at each step the min head < current sum. Since current sum increases by popped value. This is similar to "can we merge two sequences with a threshold".

Maybe we can precompute for each position K the maximal left expansion and right expansion using a stack. Let's consider the left side only. Suppose we ignore right side and only expand left. Starting with sum S, we can absorb a contiguous segment to the left if each A_j < current sum. This is like a one-sided process. For each position i, define L_i = the furthest left index reachable by expanding only left from i with initial sum A_i? But with right side, sum can increase before expanding left, allowing further left. However, the final interval is the result of alternating sides.

Could use "doubling" to accelerate expansion: For a given current sum S and starting boundary, we can find the maximal segment on one side that can be absorbed using current sum. This segment length depends on S. Since S can be large, but maybe we can precompute "next barrier" functions.

Let's define for each index i, the "left absorption capacity" f(i, S): starting with interval ending at i (so next left is i-1) and current sum S, how far left can we absorb if we only go left? This is the largest l such that for all j from i-1 down to l, A_j < S + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k. This is equivalent to the first position j where A_j >= S + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k. Rearranged: A_j + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k >= S? Wait condition to absorb j: A_j < S + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k. Let T_j = sum_{k=j}^{i-1} A_k? Then sum after absorbing j = S + T_j. The condition is A_j < S + (T_j - A_j) => 2A_j +? Actually S + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k = S + T_j - A_j. So condition A_j < S + T_j - A_j => 2A_j - T_j < S. Not a simple prefix.

Maybe define P[j]=sum_{1..j}. Then sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k = P[i-1]-P[j]. Condition A_j < S + P[i-1]-P[j] => A_j + P[j] < S + P[i-1]. Since A_j + P[j] = P[j] + A_j = P[j-1] + 2A_j. Let Q[j] = P[j] + A_j = P[j-1] + 2A_j. Then condition Q[j] < S + P[i-1]. Nice! For a fixed right boundary i-1 and current sum S, the first j (from right to left) where Q[j] >= S + P[i-1] blocks. Thus the left expansion can be found by a range maximum of Q? Specifically, we can absorb all j in [l, i-1] iff for all j in [l, i-1], Q[j] < S + P[i-1]. Since S + P[i-1] is constant during a one-sided expansion? Wait S is the initial sum before expanding left, and P[i-1] is sum of positions to the right of j up to i-1. For each j, the threshold S + P[i-1] is constant! Let's verify: Condition A_j < S + sum_{k=j+1}^{i-1} A_k. The right side is S + P[i-1] - P[j]. Rearranged A_j + P[j] < S + P[i-1]. Q[j] = A_j + P[j]. Yes, threshold constant for all j! Great. Therefore, expanding left from an interval [i, r] with current sum S (where i is left boundary, next left i-1) can absorb a contiguous block of positions down to l iff max_{j in [l, i-1]} Q[j] < S + P[i-1]. The furthest left is one before the first position to the left of i with Q[j] >= S + P[i-1]? Wait j ranges from i-1 down to l. The condition for each j uses Q[j] < S + P[i-1], where P[i-1] is fixed (sum up to original left boundary -1). So if any Q[j] >= threshold, that j blocks; you cannot absorb beyond it (since to absorb further left, you must absorb j first, which fails). Thus the maximal left expansion is until just after the nearest index j < i with Q[j] >= S + P[i-1]. That is, l = (nearest blocking index to the left of i) + 1. This is huge!

Let's test with sample. A=[4,13,2,3,2,6]. P:0,4,17,19,22,24,30. Q[j]=P[j]+A_j? P[1]+A1=4+4=8; P2+A2=17+13=30; P3+A3=19+2=21; P4+A4=22+3=25; P5+A5=24+2=26; P6+A6=30+6=36.

Similarly, expanding right from interval [l, i] with current sum S (next right i+1) can absorb a block to the right up to r iff for all j in [i+1, r], condition A_j < S + sum_{k=i+1}^{j-1} A_k. Let prefix P. Sum_{k=i+1}^{j-1} A_k = P[j-1]-P[i]. Condition A_j + ? Rearranged A_j < S + P[j-1] - P[i] => A_j + P[j-1] < S + P[i]. Let R[j] = A_j + P[j-1] = P[j]? Wait P[j]=P[j-1]+A_j, so A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j? No P[j]=P[j-1]+A_j, so A_j+P[j-1] = P[j] + (A_j? actually P[j-1]+A_j = P[j]. Wait P[j] = P[j-1]+A_j. So A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j? Let's compute: P[j] = P[j-1]+A_j. Thus A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j - A_j? No P[j-1]+A_j = P[j]. So A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j? That's wrong: P[j-1]+A_j = P[j]. So A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j? Let's do algebra: P[j-1] + A_j = P[j]. Therefore A_j + P[j-1] = P[j] + A_j? No, P[j-1]+A_j = P[j], so A_j+P[j-1] = P[j]. Yes! Wait A_j + P[j-1] = P[j]. Because P[j-1]+A_j = P[j]. So condition A_j < S + P[j-1] - P[i] => A_j + P[i] < S + P[j-1] => P[i] + A_j < S + P[j-1]. Not as clean. Let's redo carefully.

Right expansion: current interval [l, i] (right boundary i), sum S. Absorb positions i+1, i+2, ... sequentially. Before absorbing j, current sum = S + sum_{k=i+1}^{j-1} A_k = S + P[j-1] - P[i]. Condition: A_j < S + P[j-1] - P[i].
Rearrange: A_j + P[i] < S + P[j-1].
But P[j-1] = P[j] - A_j. So A_j + P[i] < S + P[j] - A_j => 2A_j + P[i] < S + P[j]. Not constant threshold.

Alternatively, define a transformed value for right side using suffix sums. Let suffix sums Suf[i]=sum_{i..N}. For right expansion, consider mirrored array. If we reverse the array, right expansion becomes left expansion. So we can handle both sides with the same Q on reversed array. Good.

For left expansion, we found a clean condition with Q[j]=P[j]+A_j and threshold S+P[i-1]. This is because the sum of elements to the right of j up to i-1 is P[i-1]-P[j], and condition A_j < S + P[i-1]-P[j] => A_j+P[j] < S+P[i-1]. Yes.

For right expansion, if we reverse indices, let B_t = A_{N+1-t}. Then expanding right in A corresponds to expanding left in B. We can precompute analogous Q_rev.

This is very useful. It means for a given interval [l,r] with sum S, we can determine in O(log N) how far we can expand left before hitting a blocker, and similarly right. Specifically:
- Left expansion from current left boundary l (next index l-1) with current sum S: Let base = P[l-1] (sum before l). The blocker condition for index j<l is Q[j] >= S + base, where Q[j]=P[j]+A_j. The nearest blocker to the left of l is the largest j < l with Q[j] >= S + base. If none, can expand to 1. The new left boundary becomes (that blocker index + 1). Because we can absorb all positions j with Q[j] < threshold; if a blocker at j has Q[j] >= threshold, we cannot absorb it, so stop at j+1.
- Right expansion from current right boundary r (next index r+1) with current sum S: Use reversed array. Let B, prefix PB, QB[j]=PB[j]+B_j. In original, the next right index corresponds to reversed index n-r. We can find nearest blocker to the right of r in original as the nearest blocker to the left in reversed. Or derive analogous with suffix sums: Let Suf[i]=sum_{i..N}. For right expansion from r, condition for j>r: A_j < S + sum_{k=r+1}^{j-1} A_k = S + (Suf[r+1]-Suf[j])? Let's derive using suffix. Let Suf[i]=sum_{i..N}. Sum_{k=r+1}^{j-1} A_k = Suf[r+1] - Suf[j]. Condition A_j < S + Suf[r+1] - Suf[j] => A_j + Suf[j] < S + Suf[r+1]. Define T[j]=A_j+Suf[j]. Then condition T[j] < S + Suf[r+1]. The nearest blocker to the right of r is the smallest j > r with T[j] >= S + Suf[r+1]. If none, can expand to N. New right boundary = blocker index - 1. Let's verify with sample. A, Suf: Suf[7]=0; Suf6=6, Suf5=8, Suf4=11, Suf3=13, Suf2=26, Suf1=30. T[j]=A_j+Suf[j]: T1=4+30=34, T2=13+26=39, T3=2+13=15, T4=3+11=14, T5=2+8=10, T6=6+6=12.
K=4, interval [4,4], S=3, r=4, Suf[r+1]=Suf5=8, threshold11. Need smallest j>4 with T[j]>=11: T5=10<11, T6=12>=11, so blocker j=6, new right boundary=5. Thus from [4,4] sum3, right expansion can absorb pos5 (2) but not pos6 (6). Indeed K=4 absorbs pos5, then sum5, pos6=6 not <5. Good.
After absorbing pos5, interval [4,5], S=5, r=5, Suf6=6, threshold11. Smallest j>5 with T[j]>=11: T6=12>=11, blocker j=6, new right boundary=5, cannot absorb pos6. But in actual K=4 after absorbing left pos3, sum7, then right pos6=6<7 absorbed. The one-sided right expansion from [4,5] with S=5 cannot absorb pos6; but after left expansion sum increases to7, then right expansion can. So we need alternate sides, but each one-sided expansion can be jumped using these blocker queries.

This is a breakthrough. We can simulate the greedy process for a single K by repeatedly:
1. Expand left as far as possible using current sum S and current l: find blocker j<l with Q[j] >= S + P[l-1]. If exists, l = j+1; else l=1. Update S = P[r]-P[l-1].
2. Expand right as far as possible using current sum S and current r: find blocker j>r with T[j] >= S + Suf[r+1]. If exists, r = j-1; else r=N. Update S.
Repeat until no expansion on either side (i.e., l unchanged and r unchanged). Since after expanding one side, S increases, the other side may become expandable. This is exactly the closure. We can jump over many absorptions at once.

Now, can we compute this for all K efficiently? The number of iterations per K might still be O(number of blockers crossed), which could be O(N) per K. But maybe with the laminar tree and this jump mechanism, we can compute all answers using divide and conquer or memoization.

Observe that the left expansion from a state depends only on l and S. The blocker index is the nearest index to the left with Q >= threshold. Since threshold = S + P[l-1]. But S = P[r]-P[l-1]. So threshold = P[r]. Wait! For an interval [l,r], current sum S = P[r]-P[l-1]. Then S + P[l-1] = P[r]. Ah! The threshold for left expansion is simply P[r]! Let's check: Left expansion from interval [l,r] with sum S: condition for j<l is Q[j] < S + P[l-1] = P[r]. So the left blocker is the nearest j < l with Q[j] >= P[r]. This depends only on r, not on l or S! Beautiful.

Similarly, right expansion threshold: S + Suf[r+1] = (P[r]-P[l-1]) + Suf[r+1]. Since Suf[r+1] = total - P[r]. So threshold = total - P[l-1]. Let's verify: S + Suf[r+1] = P[r]-P[l-1] + total - P[r] = total - P[l-1]. Thus right blocker is the nearest j > r with T[j] >= total - P[l-1], depends only on l. Great!

So the process simplifies dramatically:
- From interval [l,r], left expansion can absorb all positions down to L = (nearest index < l with Q[j] >= P[r]) + 1, where Q[j]=P[j]+A_j. If no such index, L=1.
- Right expansion can absorb all positions up to R = (nearest index > r with T[j] >= total - P[l-1]) - 1, where T[j]=A_j+Suf[j]. If no such index, R=N.

After expanding both sides, the new interval is [L,R] (if both expansions happen). But wait if we expand left first, r remains same, so P[r] same, left expansion threshold same. If we expand right first, l remains same, right expansion threshold same. The final expanded interval after doing both one-sided expansions from [l,r] is [L,R] where L determined by r and R determined by l, because thresholds depend only on the opposite boundary. Is it valid to expand left and right independently? Suppose we expand left to L, then S increases, so right expansion threshold total - P[l-1]? Wait if we expand left first, l changes to L, so the threshold for right expansion becomes total - P[L-1], which is larger than before (since P[L-1] smaller, threshold larger), making right expansion potentially go further than R computed from original l. Similarly, expanding right first changes r, making left threshold P[R] larger, allowing further left. Thus the two expansions are not independent; after expanding one side, the other side's threshold increases, enabling more expansion. However, the formulas show that the threshold for left depends on the right boundary r, and threshold for right depends on left boundary l. So expanding left changes l, which increases the right threshold, allowing further right; expanding right changes r, increasing left threshold. This is a mutual recursion:
L = f_left(r) = (prev Q blocker < l? Actually nearest j < l with Q[j] >= P[r]) + 1? Wait if we are currently at [l,r], left expansion can go until the nearest blocker to the left of l with Q >= P[r]. But after expanding right, r increases, P[r] increases, so the blocker threshold increases, meaning some previously blocking Q may become < new threshold, allowing further left. Thus the final left boundary after both sides may be determined by the final right boundary, and vice versa:
Final interval [L,R] satisfies:
L = 1 + max{ j < L? Actually if we expand left from current L? Let's define closure equations. At final interval [L,R], no further expansion possible. Wait if there is a blocker at j < L, then left boundary L is immediately to the right of the nearest blocker to the left of L with Q >= P[R]. If there is no blocker, L=1. Similarly R = -1 + min{ j > R | T[j] >= total - P[L-1] } (or N).
These are self-consistent equations.

But for simulation, we can use a loop:
l=r=K, S=A_K.
While True:
  new_l = left_expand(l, r) using P[r]
  new_r = right_expand(r, l) using total - P[l-1]
  if new_l == l and new_r == r: break
  l, r = new_l, new_r
But as noted, if we compute new_l and new_r simultaneously from old l,r, we might miss the synergy: after left expansion, right threshold increases, so new_r should be computed with new_l, not old l. So we should do sequential: expand left, update l, then expand right with updated l, update r, then expand left with updated r, etc. However, because thresholds only depend on the opposite boundary, we can alternate. For K to absorb all of [l,r], starting at K, the greedy process must eventually reach l and r. Consider the left side. Maybe the correct condition involves the greedy process: at each step, the smaller boundary must be < current sum. This is like merging two sequences. There is a known characterization using "min of prefix maxima"? Let's analyze the two-sided process more.

For fixed K and interval [l,r], define left sequence Lseq = A_{K-1}, A_{K-2}, ..., A_l (from near to far). Right sequence Rseq = A_{K+1}, ..., A_r. We have initial sum a = A_K. At each step, let x = min(current head of Lseq, current head of Rseq) (if one side exhausted, the other). If x < current sum, absorb x (pop from that side), sum += x; else fail. This is deterministic. We need know if it absorbs all.

This process is equivalent to the following: We have two stacks; we always pop the smaller top. This is exactly the algorithm to merge two sequences into one sequence sorted by the "top" order. The sequence of popped values is the "greedy merge" of the two sequences. The condition for success is that each popped value is less than the sum of all previously popped values plus a. Since sum increases by popped values.

Can we characterize success in terms of prefix sums of the merged sequence? The greedy merge order is determined solely by the two sequences, independent of sum! Because we always choose the smaller current head. So for a given K and interval, the absorption order is fixed: it's the merge of the left outward sequence and right outward sequence by always taking the smaller head. This is like merging two lists by value (not sorted globally, but the merge of two sequences by comparing heads). The resulting sequence is the "shuffle" that would be produced by merging two sorted? Actually the two sequences are arbitrary; the greedy merge by smaller head is a standard merge of two sequences according to their head values, but the heads change. This is exactly the same as merging two sequences into one sequence that is lexicographically smallest? It is the unique sequence obtained by repeatedly outputting the smaller of the two current front elements. This is known as the "greedy merge" or "shuffle merge".

Then success condition: For the resulting sequence b_1, b_2, ..., b_m (m = length-1), we need b_t < a + sum_{s<t} b_s for all t. This is the multiset condition but with the specific greedy merge order. Since the greedy merge chooses the smallest available boundary, it should be the optimal order among all valid interleavings? We argued absorbing the smaller boundary is safe and optimal. Is the greedy merge order the best possible? If a smaller boundary is absorbable, absorbing it maximizes sum and doesn't hurt. If the smaller boundary is not absorbable, then no boundary is absorbable (since other >= smaller), so fail. Thus the greedy merge order is indeed the only order that can succeed; any successful order must at each step absorb the smaller boundary if it is absorbable? Suppose smaller boundary x >= current sum, but larger boundary y could be < current sum? Impossible since y >= x >= current sum. So if smaller not absorbable, no action. If smaller absorbable, absorbing it is safe. Therefore the process is deterministic: always absorb the smaller boundary. Thus the absorption order is fixed by the two sequences. Great! This means for each K and interval, success is simply checking the greedy merge sequence condition.

Now, can we compute for all K the maximal interval using this deterministic merge? Maybe we can view the process as a path in a grid: state (l,r). If equal, both same value; choosing either yields same sum and next state? If equal, the next boundary on one side may differ, so future could differ. But if equal and both < current sum, both are absorbable; does choice matter? Earlier we thought any available action is safe, but if equal, choosing left vs right could lead to different future because the next head on that side may be smaller/larger. Is one choice better? This changes order but both equal heads eventually absorbed. Is the final reachable set independent? We argued monotonicity ensures any available action is safe. Since both equal heads are available, absorbing one cannot prevent absorbing the other. Thus final closure is independent. For deterministic simulation, we can break ties arbitrarily, e.g., prefer left. The greedy merge with tie left is fine.

Now, the greedy merge sequence for a given K and interval can be generated by comparing A_{l-1} and A_{r+1}. Thus the problem reduces to: For each starting position K, simulate the deterministic process:
- Maintain interval [l,r], sum S = P[r]-P[l-1].
- At each step, let x = A_{l-1} if l>1 else INF, y = A_{r+1} if r<N else INF.
- If min(x,y) < S, absorb the side with smaller value (tie arbitrary), update l/r and S.
- Else stop. Answer S.

This is exactly the original but with the rule "always absorb smaller boundary". We knew this. The key is the absorption order is fixed by comparing boundary values, independent of S except for stopping.

The current interval before adding v contains all vertices with bottleneck < M? Not exactly, but Prim's order is known to be nondecreasing in the minimax distance. Since the candidate key is the vertex weight, but a vertex with low weight behind a high-weight vertex cannot be added until the high-weight vertex is added; its bottleneck is the high weight. So it will be added after all vertices with bottleneck less than that high weight. Thus the order is by bottleneck value.

Therefore, the greedy expansion order from K is exactly the order of vertices sorted by their "maximum A on the path from K" (bottleneck), with ties in some order. This bottleneck view might help with the sum condition. To add a vertex v with bottleneck M, before adding v, the current interval contains all vertices with bottleneck < M? Actually Prim adds vertices in nondecreasing bottleneck; so when the next vertex has bottleneck M, the current interval is exactly the connected component of K in the subgraph of vertices with bottleneck < M? Since vertices with bottleneck < M are exactly those in the same component of the subgraph induced by vertices with A < M? Let's define subgraph of vertices with A < M. Since bottleneck < M iff all vertices on path from K to v have A < M, i.e., v is in the connected component of K in the set {i | A_i < M}. Wait vertices with A < M that are connected to K without passing through A >= M. This is exactly the component of K in the subgraph induced by A < M. This component is an interval [L_M, R_M] containing K, bounded by vertices with A >= M (or array ends). Prim's algorithm adds vertices in increasing bottleneck M, so after processing all vertices with bottleneck < M, the current interval is exactly [L_M, R_M]. Then the next vertices to add are the boundary vertices of this component, which have A >= M. Their bottleneck is M (because path max M). Among the two boundaries, Prim adds the one with smaller A (since candidate key is A). After adding one boundary vertex of value M, the component expands to include it, and may expose more vertices with A < M? Wait if the added boundary has value M, then vertices beyond it with A < M now have bottleneck M (path max M), so they should be added at the same bottleneck level M, before any vertex with bottleneck > M. But Prim's candidate key for those beyond vertices is their own A (< M), so once the boundary of value M is added, the next vertex beyond has key < M, so Prim will add it immediately, even though its bottleneck is M. Thus the order within the same bottleneck level M is: add a boundary vertex of value M, then possibly a run of vertices with A < M behind it, then maybe another boundary of value M, etc. This is exactly the process of expanding through "gates" of value M into regions of lower values.

This suggests a hierarchical decomposition by values. The component of vertices with A < X expands as X increases. The bottleneck levels are the values of "barrier" vertices. The boundary vertices with A = M (or >= M?) are the gates to enter adjacent components of A < M? Actually if boundary has value M, after crossing it, you enter a region of vertices with A < M that were not connected to K before because the gate blocked them. These vertices have bottleneck M, so they are added at level M. Then you may encounter another gate of value M, etc. This is like flood fill through barriers of increasing height.

Now, the sum condition: We stop when the next vertex to add (the smaller boundary) has value >= current sum. Since the next vertex's value is the current boundary value. In terms of bottleneck levels, we process vertices in Prim order; current sum is sum of added vertices. We stop at the first vertex whose value >= current sum. This is similar to "can absorb all vertices with bottleneck <= X" if the sum condition holds.

This is reminiscent of "flood fill with increasing water level" where water level is S. The process can be seen as: current interval is a "lake" with water level S. Let's model as terrain heights A_i, starting at source K with water volume? Actually water level S is the sum of heights of flooded cells, not a maximum. When flooding a cell of height h < S, the water level increases by h. This is like the total mass of flooded cells. It is not a standard water level (which would be max height), but since h < S, the new S = S+h > S. It can flood cells with height up to S.

This is exactly the "slime" process. For a single source, we can simulate with a priority queue of boundary cells by height: pop smallest height h; if h < S, add it, S+=h; else stop. This is the same as Prim order. This is a known algorithm for "can eat all" and can be implemented O(N log N) for one source. After exhausting the subtree of K (or stopping), the next vertices to consider are in the sibling subtrees of ancestors. To move from K to a vertex outside its subtree, you must cross an ancestor u with value A_u > A_K. The bottleneck is A_u. The order among outside vertices is by increasing ancestor value as you go up the tree: first the sibling subtree of K's parent? Let's define max-Cartesian tree. K is a node. Its parent p has value >= A_K. The vertices in the subtree of p but outside K's subtree have LCA with K = p, bottleneck A_p. These are the "sibling" subtrees of K and its ancestors. To reach them, you must cross p (the parent), which is a boundary between K's current interval and the sibling. In the array, p is located either to the left or right of K's subtree; crossing p (value A_p) opens up the sibling subtree on the other side of p. After crossing p, you can absorb the sibling subtree (whose values are <= A_p) before crossing higher ancestors. Thus the Prim order from K is:
1. Traverse the subtree of K in some order (all bottleneck A_K).
2. Then for each ancestor u of K from bottom to top (parent, grandparent, ...), after crossing u, traverse the "other" subtrees attached to u (the child of u that does not contain K) in some order, all with bottleneck A_u.
This is exactly a depth-first traversal of the max-Cartesian tree rooted at K? More precisely, if we consider the max-Cartesian tree and perform a DFS starting from K, where at each node we visit the node itself and then children? But the bottleneck order is by node value: visit K, then its descendants (value <= A_K), then parent (value >= A_K), then parent's other child subtree, then grandparent, etc. This is like an Euler tour of the tree where we go up and down.

This tree structure may allow us to compute the sum condition efficiently. The sum condition: We stop when the next value in this order is >= current sum. Since the order is deterministic, the final interval is a prefix of this traversal. The answer is the sum of that prefix.

K=2 ancestors: pos2, root. C(pos2 [1,2])? Starting pos2=25, left pos1=22<25 sum47, yes. C(root)? After [1,2] sum47, right pos3=61>47, cannot. Highest pos2. Output47.
K=1 ancestors: pos1,pos2,root. C(pos1)={1}; C(pos2)? Starting pos1=22, right pos2=25>22, cannot. Highest pos1. Output22.

After [7,12] sum59, left pos6=37<59 sum96, left pos5=21<96 sum117, left pos4=10<96 sum127, yes. Root? left pos3=61<127 sum188, left pos2=25<188 sum213, left pos1=22<188 sum235, yes. Highest root. This is a major insight: Final interval for K is the highest ancestor of K in the max-Cartesian tree such that K can absorb the entire subtree of that ancestor. Equivalently, if we define for each node u the set C(u) of positions in its subtree that can absorb the whole subtree u, then for any K, the final interval is the node u = highest ancestor of K with K ∈ C(u). Since ancestors are ordered by tree depth, we can find the highest (closest to root) such ancestor.

Now, how to compute C(u) for all nodes in the max-Cartesian tree efficiently? The subtree of u is a contiguous interval [l_u, r_u] where u is the maximum. C(u) is the set of positions K in [l_u, r_u] such that starting at K, Takahashi can absorb all elements in [l_u, r_u]. We need for each original position K, the highest ancestor u with K ∈ C(u). This is like marking each node u with the set C(u), and for each position K, we need the highest ancestor whose set contains K. Equivalently, we can propagate K upward until it fails.

Can we compute C(u) using the max-Cartesian tree children? Let's derive recurrence for C(u) in terms of children. Let u have left child L (subtree [l_u, u-1]) and right child R (subtree [u+1, r_u]). The interval of u is L ∪ {u} ∪ R. For a position K in the subtree of u, to absorb all of u's interval, K must first absorb its own child subtree? Actually if K is in L, then to absorb all of u's interval, K must be able to absorb the entire left child subtree L (which contains K) and then cross u to absorb R. But C(L) is the set of positions that can absorb L. If K ∈ C(L), then starting at K can absorb L, ending with sum S_L = sum(L). Then the next element to absorb is u (the root of the parent interval), which is adjacent to L. To absorb u, need S_L > A_u. If yes, after absorbing u, sum becomes S_L + A_u = sum(L)+A_u. Then to absorb R, need to absorb the entire right child subtree R from the outside. This is not the same as C(R) (which is starting from inside R). We need define "externally absorbable" of a subtree: Given an incoming sum S, can we absorb all elements of a subtree interval from one side? But since the subtree is contiguous and adjacent to the current interval at its boundary, absorbing it from outside is a one-sided process. However, if we have just absorbed u, the current interval includes L and u, and R is adjacent at its left boundary (if R is right child) or right boundary. To absorb R, we need to absorb the elements of R in the order from the boundary inward. This is exactly the reverse of the process that forms R? We need a notion of "can absorb interval from outside with current sum S".

But maybe the max-Cartesian tree structure simplifies external absorption. The subtree R is an interval where the maximum is A_Rroot. To absorb R from outside, the first element encountered is the boundary element of R (the leftmost if R is to the right). This boundary element is some node in R, not necessarily the root. The process of absorbing R from outside is equivalent to starting at that boundary element? Actually if we have current sum S, and we absorb the boundary elements of R one by one, this is exactly the reverse of the peeling process for R, but with an external initial sum S instead of starting from an internal K. We need to know for each subtree and each possible incoming sum S whether it can be fully absorbed. This seems like a function.

However, maybe we don't need external absorption if we use the highest ancestor approach differently. For K to be in C(u), the process from K absorbs the entire interval [l_u, r_u]. This is the original process, not decomposed by tree. But we can compute C(u) recursively using the Prim order within the subtree. The Prim order starting from K within the interval [l_u, r_u] is the same as the Prim order in the whole array restricted to that interval, because the boundary values outside are larger (ancestors) and not considered. The max-Cartesian tree of the interval is the subtree rooted at u. So C(u) is exactly the set of starts K in this subtree such that the Prim process within this tree (with all values <= A_u) absorbs all nodes before failing.

Maybe there is a simpler way to compute the highest absorbable ancestor for each position using a bottom-up DP on the max-Cartesian tree. Let's define for each node u:
- sum[u] = sum of A in subtree u.
- "good" positions K in subtree u that can absorb all of subtree u: C(u).
We want for each leaf/position K, the highest ancestor u with K ∈ C(u).

Can we compute C(u) from C(L), C(R), and the ability to absorb the other child from outside? Let's derive.

Let u have left child L and right child R (one may be empty). The interval is L, u, R. Suppose K ∈ L and K ∈ C(L). Starting at K, we can absorb all of L, sum becomes S_L. Now the current interval is L. The adjacent elements are u (to the right) and possibly the left boundary of L? But L's left boundary is the interval boundary, outside is ancestor, not in u's interval. To absorb all of u's interval, we need to absorb u and then R (or maybe absorb R before u? u is between L and R, so must absorb u before R). Condition to absorb u: A_u < S_L. After absorbing u, sum = S_L + A_u. Then we need to absorb R from outside with current sum S_L + A_u. So we need a predicate Ext(R, S) = can absorb all of R given initial sum S, approaching from the left (since R is to the right of u). Similarly, if K ∈ R, need absorb R first, then u, then Ext(L, S_R + A_u) from the right.

Thus C(u) = 
{ K ∈ C(L) | A_u < S_L and Ext(R, S_L + A_u) } 
∪ { K ∈ C(R) | A_u < S_R and Ext(L, S_R + A_u) }
∪ maybe {u}? Can K = u absorb all of u's interval? Starting at u, need absorb L and R. The order is determined by smaller boundary (left boundary of L? Actually adjacent to u are the rightmost element of L and leftmost element of R). This is not simply C(L) or C(R). But u itself may be in C(u) if it can absorb both children. In examples, root direct positions often include u? Sample1 root pos2=13 is in C(root) yes. Node pos6=6 is in C(pos6) yes. Node pos4=3? Is pos4 in C(pos4 [3,5])? Starting at pos4=3, can absorb pos3=2 and pos5=2, yes. So u is often in C(u) if it can absorb children. So we need include u.

For K = u, starting at u, to absorb all, we need to absorb L and R in some order. The first absorption is the smaller of the boundary elements of L and R. This is exactly the original process within the two child subtrees. We can view it as: absorb one child subtree (or part) then the other. This is more complex but maybe can be handled by a recursive function.

Maybe there is an even simpler characterization of C(u): K ∈ C(u) iff A_K is the maximum? No, sample node pos6 [3,6] C={4,6}; K=4 value3, not max (max6). So no.

Let's think about the "highest absorbable ancestor" from the perspective of each position K. As we go up the max-Cartesian tree, the interval expands by adding ancestors. The process from K to a higher ancestor u requires crossing a sequence of ancestors. The condition to cross an ancestor p (from child c to parent p) is that after absorbing the current interval (which is the subtree of c or higher), the sum S is > A_p. Since A_p is the maximum of the parent interval, and the current interval is the child's subtree. Thus to move from child c to parent p, we need sum(c) > A_p? Wait if K can absorb the entire child subtree c, the sum after absorbing c is S_c = sum(c). Then to absorb p, need S_c > A_p. But if K cannot absorb the entire child subtree c, it cannot reach p at all. Therefore, the highest absorbable ancestor for K is determined by a chain: Starting at node K (leaf), K can absorb its own subtree (singleton) trivially. To go up to parent p, K must be able to absorb the entire current node c (which is the highest absorbable so far), and then absorb p, and then absorb the sibling subtree of p from outside. But absorbing the sibling subtree is necessary to claim K ∈ C(p). However, maybe if K can absorb c and A_p < sum(c), then after absorbing p, the sum becomes sum(c)+A_p. To absorb the sibling subtree, we need to absorb it from outside. Is it always possible if the sibling subtree's maximum is < sum(c)+A_p? Not necessarily, but maybe due to the max-Cartesian tree property, if the incoming sum is greater than the maximum of the sibling subtree, then the entire sibling subtree can be absorbed? Let's test.

Suppose we approach a subtree R from outside with initial sum S. If S > max(R), can we always absorb all of R? The first boundary element of R has value <= max(R) < S, so absorb it. Sum increases. The next boundary element also <= max(R) < S (since S increased), so absorb it. By induction, if S > max(R), then every boundary element encountered is <= max(R) < current sum, so we can absorb all of R! Is that true? The current sum only increases, so it remains > max(R). Thus yes! This is huge. External absorption of a subtree is guaranteed if the incoming sum is greater than the maximum value in that subtree. Because all elements in the subtree are <= max, and current sum > max at all times. The order doesn't matter; any boundary element is < current sum. Therefore, to absorb a sibling subtree R after crossing p, we only need S > max(R). Since max(R) <= A_p? In a max-Cartesian tree, all descendants have value <= A_p, so max(R) <= A_p. If S > A_p, then S > max(R). Thus after absorbing p, if S_before > A_p, then new sum S_before + A_p > A_p >= max(R), so the sibling subtree can be absorbed completely! This is the key.

Let's verify with examples. Sample1 K=4, current node pos4 [3,5] sum7, parent pos6 A=6. To go up to pos6, need S=7 > A_p=6, yes. After absorbing pos6, sum13, sibling? pos6 has left child pos4 (already absorbed) and right child none? Actually pos6's subtree [3,6] includes left child pos4 and right none. So no sibling. To go up to root pos2 A=13, need S=13 > 13? false, so cannot. Good.
Sample2 K=5, current node pos5 [4,5] sum31, parent pos6 A=37, need31>37 false, cannot. K=8, current node pos8 [7,11] sum35, parent pos12 A=24, need35>24 yes. After absorbing pos12, sum59, sibling of pos8? pos12's left child is pos8 (current), right child none? Actually pos12 has left child pos8, no right child. So no sibling. Then parent pos6 A=37, need current sum59>37 yes. Absorb pos6, sum96, sibling of pos6? pos6's left child pos5 [4,5] (not absorbed yet) has max21 <96, so can absorb. Then parent root pos3 A=61, need sum? After absorbing pos5, sum117? Wait let's simulate: K=8 absorbed pos8 subtree [7,11] sum35, then pos12 sum59, then pos6 sum96, then sibling pos5 subtree [4,5] sum21 ->117, then parent root pos3 A=61, need117>61, absorb, sibling pos2 [1,2] max25 <178, absorb all. Yes.

Thus the condition to climb from a node c to its parent p is simply: the sum of the subtree c (which is the current absorbed interval) > A_p. Because after absorbing p, the sibling subtree (if any) is automatically absorbable since its max <= A_p < new sum. Wait what if the sibling subtree has max > A_p? In a max-Cartesian tree, parent p is the maximum of the entire interval, so all descendants including sibling have value <= A_p. So yes.

This is a massive simplification! Let's formalize:

In the max-Cartesian tree, each node u corresponds to a contiguous interval I_u, with A_u = max_{i in I_u} A_i. For any K in I_u, if K can absorb all of I_u (i.e., K ∈ C(u)), then the sum after absorbing I_u is sum[u]. To expand to the parent p of u, the next element to absorb is p (since p is adjacent to I_u? Is p always adjacent to I_u? In the Cartesian tree, p is the maximum of a larger interval I_p = I_u ∪ {p} ∪ I_sibling, and p is located immediately to the left or right of I_u? Actually in a Cartesian tree with inorder, if p is parent of u, then u is either left or right child. If u is left child of p, then I_u is the left part of I_p, and p is immediately to the right of I_u. If u is right child, p is immediately to the left of I_u. Thus p is adjacent to the interval I_u. Good.)
To absorb p, need current sum S = sum[u] > A_p. If true, after absorbing p, sum becomes sum[u]+A_p. The other child subtree of p (sibling) has all values <= A_p < new sum, so it can be absorbed completely. Therefore, if K can absorb I_u and sum[u] > A_p, then K can absorb I_p. Conversely, if K can absorb I_p, then it must be able to absorb I_u (the child containing K) first, and then p, so sum[u] > A_p is necessary. Thus:

C(p) contains C(u) (the positions in child u that can absorb I_u) if and only if sum[u] > A_p. Additionally, p itself is in C(p) if? Starting at p, to absorb I_p, p is the maximum. It can absorb the child subtrees. The condition to absorb a child subtree c from p is A_p > max(c)? Since A_p is max, and current sum = A_p. To absorb the first element of child c, need A_p > boundary value. Since boundary value <= max(c) <= A_p. If A_p > max(c), then yes. If A_p == max(c) (duplicate maximum), strict condition may fail for that boundary element if it equals A_p. But duplicates require careful tie-breaking. If all values in child are < A_p, then p can absorb both children and thus p ∈ C(p). If there is a descendant with value equal to A_p, then the boundary might be equal and not absorbable initially. But can p absorb the other child first to increase sum, then absorb the equal? If p absorbs one child, sum increases, then the equal value in the other child may become absorbable. So p might still be in C(p). Need handle duplicates.

Let's first assume all A_i distinct, or use tie-breaking in Cartesian tree to avoid equal boundary issues. In competitive programming, duplicates can be handled by tie-breaking (e.g., choose leftmost maximum) and strict inequality. But if equal values exist, the max-Cartesian tree with tie-breaking ensures parent value >= child, but equal possible. The condition sum[child] > A_parent is still necessary to climb from child to parent. For p itself, starting at p, to absorb a child subtree, the first boundary element might be less than A_p if tie-breaking places equal values not at boundary? Need analyze.

Let's test duplicates. A=[2,2], N=2. Max-Cartesian tree with tie-breaking leftmost max: root pos1=2, right child pos2=2. For K=1, starting sum2, right neighbor2<2 false, final [1,1] sum2. C(root [1,2]) should not contain1. Condition to climb from child pos2 to parent pos1: sum[pos2]=2 > A_parent=2? false, so C(pos2) not propagated to root. Good. For K=2, C(pos2)={2}; parent pos1, sum2 >2 false, so not root. Final [2,2]. What about p=pos1 itself? Starting at pos1, sum2, right child pos2=2 not absorbable, so p not in C(root). Our rule "p in C(p) if A_p > max(child)" would fail because max(child)=2 equal. So p not in C(p). Good. If A=[2,1,2], max-Cartesian tie leftmost root pos1=2, right child pos3=2 with left child pos2=1? Actually interval [2,3] max pos3=2, left child pos2=1. Root pos1 right child pos3. K=1 sum2, right pos2=1<2 absorb sum3, right pos3=2<3 absorb all, so C(root) contains1. p=pos1 in C(root) despite equal max in right subtree, because the boundary of right subtree is pos2=1 < A_p, not the equal pos3. Thus p can absorb child subtree even if max(child)=A_p, as long as the boundary element is smaller. So p ∈ C(p) condition is not simply max(child) < A_p; it depends on the boundary of the child subtree. However, for climbing from a child u to parent p, we assumed K has absorbed the entire child u, so the boundary to p is p itself, not an element of u. The condition sum[u] > A_p is necessary and sufficient to absorb p. This holds regardless of duplicates. After absorbing p, the sibling subtree may have max <= A_p, and current sum > A_p, so it is absorbable. Thus propagation from child C(u) to parent C(p) is valid with condition sum[u] > A_p.

Now, what about p itself being in C(p)? This is a base case for the node p. We can compute it separately: Starting at p, can p absorb all of I_p? Since p is the maximum (by tie-breaking), the initial sum is A_p. It needs to absorb its left child subtree and right child subtree (if any). The first absorption will be the smaller of the boundary elements adjacent to p: the rightmost element of left child (if exists) and the leftmost element of right child (if exists). If no children, p ∈ C(p) trivially. If there are children, p can absorb a child subtree if the boundary element < current sum. After absorbing some elements, sum increases. This is exactly the same problem but starting at the root of the subtree. We could compute p ∈ C(p) by simulating the process within I_p, but maybe there is a simple condition using the tree: p can absorb the entire I_p iff it can absorb at least one child subtree (or both) and then the other. Since p is the maximum, any element in a child subtree is <= A_p. The only obstacle is that the first boundary element might be equal to A_p (if duplicate at boundary). If both boundary elements are >= A_p? They cannot be > A_p, so they are = A_p if not absorbable. If a boundary element equals A_p, p cannot absorb it initially. But it might absorb the other side first if that boundary < A_p, increasing sum, then absorb the equal. If both boundary elements equal A_p and no other side, p cannot absorb either, so p not in C(p). If one side has boundary < A_p, p can start there.

But for the highest ancestor propagation, we don't necessarily need to know C(p) for p itself? For a position K that is the node p, its final interval is the highest ancestor u with p ∈ C(u). We need to know which ancestors contain p. This can be computed by starting at node p and climbing: p ∈ C(p) if base true. Then if p ∈ C(p) and sum[p] > A_parent, then p ∈ C(parent), etc. So we need base C(p) for each node p (whether the node itself can absorb its own subtree).

Can we compute base C(u) for each node u efficiently? Let's denote self_ok[u] = whether starting at position u (the maximum of interval I_u) can absorb all of I_u. If self_ok[u] is true, then u belongs to C(u). Then propagation upward: For any node u, C(u) is the union of:
- {u} if self_ok[u].
- For each child c of u, if sum[c] > A_u, then all positions in C(c) are also in C(u). Because if a position in child c can absorb I_c, and sum[c] > A_u, then it can absorb u and the sibling subtree.
Is that sufficient? What about a position in child c that cannot absorb I_c but could absorb I_u? Impossible, because to absorb I_u it must first absorb I_c (the part containing it) before crossing u. So must be in C(c). Thus the recurrence is exact! This is beautiful.

Excellent! So the algorithm is:

1. Build the max-Cartesian tree of the array A. Need tie-breaking consistent. The tree has N nodes, each node i has left child and right child, parent. The interval of node i is [L_i, R_i] (subtree range). Since it's a Cartesian tree with inorder indices, the subtree of i is contiguous and i is the maximum (by tie-breaking) in that interval.

2. Compute for each node i:
   - sum[i] = sum of A in its subtree (interval). This can be computed during Cartesian tree construction or via prefix sums after knowing L_i,R_i: sum[i] = pref[R_i] - pref[L_i-1].
   - self_ok[i] = whether starting at position i can absorb the entire subtree interval of i.
   - C[i]? We don't need store full sets. We need for each original position K, find the highest ancestor u such that K ∈ C(u). The recurrence:
     C(u) = {u if self_ok[u]} ∪ ⋃_{child c of u, sum[c] > A_u} C(c).
   This means membership propagates upward from a node c to its parent u iff sum[c] > A_u. If true, all positions in C(c) are also in C(u). If false, none of C(c) propagate to u or any ancestor? Wait if C(c) doesn't propagate to u, can it propagate to u's parent through a different path? Maybe we can define for each node u, not just C(u), but the final interval for each position in the subtree of u. This is recursive. The max-Cartesian tree gives a divide-and-conquer: For a node u (maximum of interval I_u), any position K in the left child subtree L, to reach u or beyond, it must first absorb all of L. If K cannot absorb all of L, its final interval lies entirely within L. If it can absorb all of L, then it can try to absorb u if sum[L] > A_u. If no, it stops at the boundary of L, i.e., its final interval is L (the entire left child subtree) if K ∈ C(L). For node u, starting at position u, the process within the interval I_u (before considering ancestors) is:
- If u has no children: ans[u] = A_u (cannot absorb anything outside? Actually outside I_u are ancestors with larger/equal values; but within I_u no other elements. However, u might absorb ancestors if conditions, but that's handled by propagation. For the base final interval within I_u, it's just u.)
- If u has children, it can absorb elements from left child L and right child R. Since u is the maximum of I_u, all elements in L and R are <= A_u. Starting sum = A_u. It will absorb the smaller of the two boundary elements adjacent to u: the rightmost element of L (if L exists) and the leftmost element of R (if R exists). If the smaller boundary is >= A_u (i.e., equal to A_u), then u cannot absorb anything (unless the other boundary is smaller? The smaller boundary would be the minimum of the two; if min >= A_u, both >= A_u, but since values <= A_u, both equal A_u, so no absorption). If one boundary < A_u, it absorbs it, sum increases, and then may continue. This is exactly the original process but within the interval I_u. We could simulate this process for each u? In worst case O(N^2) if many nodes.

If a child subtree is nonempty, its boundary adjacent to u is the closest element to u in that child's interval. In the Cartesian tree, if u has a left child, the rightmost element of the left child's interval is the boundary. If u has a right child, the leftmost element of the right child's interval is the boundary.

Now, what about a position K that is not the maximum of its node? But every position is a node; "not maximum of its node" doesn't apply. In the Cartesian tree, each position is a node, and its subtree interval is the maximal interval where it is the maximum (by tie-breaking). The algorithm computes ans for each node based on self_ok and propagation. We need prove this gives the correct final interval for every position.

Let's formalize the algorithm:

1. Build max-Cartesian tree of A with tie-breaking (e.g., if equal, choose the leftmost maximum as root of any interval). This ensures each node's value is >= all values in its subtree, and the tree is binary with inorder indices.

2. For each node i:
   - Determine its subtree interval [L_i, R_i]. In a Cartesian tree, this can be obtained during construction: L_i = leftmost index in subtree, R_i = rightmost.
   - sum[i] = pref[R_i] - pref[L_i-1].
   - Determine children left[i], right[i].
   - self_ok[i] = (left[i]==0 and right[i]==0) OR (boundary value of left child < A_i) OR (boundary value of right child < A_i).
     Boundary value of left child: if left[i] exists, the rightmost element in the left child's subtree, i.e., A[R_leftchild]? Actually the boundary between i and its left child is the rightmost element of the left child's interval. Since the left child's interval is [L_i, i-1], its rightmost is i-1. Wait in a Cartesian tree, if i has a left child, the left child's subtree covers exactly [L_i, i-1], so its rightmost element is i-1. Therefore the boundary value adjacent to i on the left is simply A[i-1]! Is that always true? If i has a left child, the left child's interval is [L_i, i-1], so yes the rightmost element is i-1. Thus boundary value = A[i-1]. Similarly, if i has a right child, its interval is [i+1, R_i], leftmost is i+1, boundary value = A[i+1]. This is much simpler! So self_ok[i] = no children OR (left child exists and A[i-1] < A[i]) OR (right child exists and A[i+1] < A[i]). But if left child exists, i-1 is in its subtree, and since i is max, A[i-1] <= A[i]. So the condition is just whether the immediate neighbor on a side that is in the subtree is strictly smaller. If both children exist, need at least one immediate neighbor < A_i. If one child exists, need that neighbor < A_i. If no children, true. This matches the earlier boundary observation.

   Wait, is it possible for i to have a left child but i-1 not in the left child's subtree? In a Cartesian tree with inorder, the left child's subtree is exactly all indices less than i within i's subtree, and since the subtree is contiguous ending at i-1, yes i-1 is included. So boundary is i-1. Good.

3. Compute ans[i]:
   - If self_ok[i] is false: ans[i] = A[i] (cannot absorb anything in its subtree).
   - If self_ok[i] is true: ans[i] = sum[v] where v is the highest ancestor reachable from i by following parent pointers while sum[current_node] > A[parent_node]. More precisely, start cur = i. While parent[cur] exists and sum[cur] > A[parent[cur]]: cur = parent[cur]. Then ans[i] = sum[cur].
   Because if self_ok[i] true, i can absorb its entire subtree, sum=sum[i]. If sum[i] > A[parent], it can absorb parent and the sibling subtree, so it can absorb the parent's entire subtree, sum=sum[parent]. Then repeat.

Now we need implement efficiently.

Steps:

- Build max-Cartesian tree with tie-breaking. Need choose tie-breaking such that parent value >= child and the "nearest greater" property holds. Standard Cartesian tree for maximum with tie-breaking: when equal, we need decide which is parent. The condition for self_ok and propagation uses A[parent] and strict >. If equal values, parent value can equal child. The stop condition sum[current] > A[parent] uses strict; if sum > equal parent, can climb. This matches strict absorption: to absorb parent of equal value, need current sum > A_parent. Good.

How to build Cartesian tree in O(N)? Standard stack algorithm for max Cartesian tree with tie-breaking. We need ensure the tree is correct for our interval/subtree property. There are two common tie-breaks: if equal, either leftmost or rightmost maximum can be root. The algorithm using a stack maintaining decreasing values (strictly decreasing?) will produce a tree where parent is >= child. Let's define.

For max Cartesian tree, process indices left to right, maintain stack with decreasing A values (strictly decreasing? If we pop while A[stack.top] < A[i] (strict), then equal values keep previous as ancestor, producing leftmost maximum as root for equal intervals. If we pop while <=, then equal values make new node ancestor, producing rightmost maximum. We need choose one. Let's choose leftmost maximum (pop while < A[i]) so that for equal values, the earlier one is parent. This gives parent value >= child, and for equal, parent is to the left. Does this maintain the property that the subtree interval boundaries are parents? Yes.

Let's test A=[2,2,2] with pop while <:
i=1 stack [1].
i=2 A2=2, while A[1] < 2? 2<2 false, so 2 is right child of 1? Standard: when not popping, new node becomes right child of stack top. So parent[2]=1, right[1]=2.
i=3 A3=2, stack [1,2], top A2=2 <2 false, parent[3]=2, right[2]=3.
Tree: 1->right2->right3. Leftmost max root, as before. Good.

For A=[2,1,2]:
i=1 stack[1].
i=2 A2=1, top2<1 false, parent[2]=1, right[1]=2.
i=3 A3=2, while top A2=1<2 pop 2. Now stack [1], A1=2<2 false. The popped node 2 becomes left child of 3? Standard algorithm: when popping, last popped becomes left child of new node. Then new node 3 becomes right child of stack top 1. Tree: 1 right 3, 3 left 2. Matches leftmost max (root pos1, right subtree max pos3, pos3 left pos2). Good.

So standard stack algorithm with pop while A[top] < A[i] (strict) yields leftmost max tie-break.

Implementation of Cartesian tree:
Initialize arrays left=[0]*(N+1), right=[0]*(N+1), parent=[0]*(N+1).
stack=[]
for i in 1..N:
    last=0
    while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:
        last = stack.pop()
    if stack:
        parent[i] = stack[-1]
        right[stack[-1]] = i
    if last:
        parent[last] = i
        left[i] = last
    stack.append(i)
Need be careful: If stack not empty, and we set right[stack[-1]]=i, but if stack[-1] already had a right child that was popped, the last popped becomes left child of i, and i becomes right child of stack[-1]. This is standard. Also if last=0, no left child. If stack empty, i is root. Need update parent of previous right child? The standard algorithm:
for i:
    last = 0
    while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:
        last = stack.pop()
    if stack:
        parent[i] = stack[-1]
        right[stack[-1]] = i
    if last:
        parent[last] = i
        left[i] = last
    stack.append(i)
This works. At the end, root is stack[0]? Actually stack contains right spine; root is stack[0] (bottom). parent[root]=0.

Need compute subtree intervals [L_i, R_i]. Since Cartesian tree is binary with inorder, the subtree of i is contiguous. We can compute L_i and R_i via DFS:
- L[i] = i if no left child else L[left[i]]
- R[i] = i if no right child else R[right[i]]
But we also need sum[i] = pref[R[i]] - pref[L[i]-1]. We can compute L,R by iterative postorder to avoid recursion depth (N 5e5). Or compute during construction? Easier: after building tree, do an iterative DFS from root to compute L,R. Since tree is binary, we can do a stack:
order = []
stack=[root]
while stack: pop, order.append, push children. Then process reversed order to compute L,R:
L[i]=i; R[i]=i
for i in reversed(order):
    if left[i]: L[i] = L[left[i]]
    if right[i]: R[i] = R[right[i]]
Because children processed before parent. Then sum[i] = pref[R[i]] - pref[L[i]-1].

But we can also compute sum[i] by postorder sum: sum[i] = A[i] + sum[left] + sum[right]. This avoids prefix and L,R? We still need L,R? For self_ok, we only need children and A[i±1]? Actually self_ok condition uses whether left/right child exists and A[i-1] < A[i] / A[i+1] < A[i]. Since if left child exists, boundary is i-1; if right child exists, boundary is i+1. So we don't need L,R for self_ok. For propagation, we need sum[i] (subtree sum). We can compute sum[i] via postorder: sum[i] = A[i] + sum[left[i]] + sum[right[i]]. This is simpler. No need prefix or L,R. Let's verify: sum of subtree interval equals sum of node plus sums of left and right child subtrees, because the subtree interval is exactly left subtree ∪ {i} ∪ right subtree. Yes. So we can compute sum by postorder.

Need compute self_ok[i]:
- If left[i]==0 and right[i]==0: True.
- Else:
   ok = False
   if left[i] != 0 and A[i-1] < A[i]: ok = True
   if right[i] != 0 and A[i+1] < A[i]: ok = True
   self_ok[i] = ok
Is it possible left[i]!=0 but i-1 is not in left subtree? No, as argued. But let's double-check with tie-breaking leftmost max. For node i, if left child exists, the left child's subtree interval is [L_i, i-1], so i-1 is in it. Thus boundary is i-1. If right child exists, boundary is i+1. Good.

Now, propagation:
We need for each node i, if self_ok[i] true, find highest ancestor cur such that for every edge along path, sum[child] > A[parent]. Since sum[child] is fixed. This is like we have a tree with directed edges from child to parent "open" if sum[child] > A[parent]. For each node i with self_ok true, ans[i] = sum[highest reachable ancestor via open edges]. If self_ok false, ans[i] = A[i] (cannot absorb its subtree; final singleton). But wait if self_ok false, could it still absorb an ancestor without absorbing its subtree? No, because to reach ancestor, it must absorb its subtree first. Since it cannot absorb its own subtree, it stops at itself. Could it absorb some elements of its subtree but not all, resulting in a final interval larger than singleton but smaller than subtree? We argued if self_ok false, both adjacent child boundaries (if any) equal A_i, so no absorption at all. What if self_ok false because only one child exists and its boundary equals A_i; no absorption. If two children both boundaries equal A_i; no absorption. So final singleton. Good.

Now, for self_ok true nodes, we need compute highest reachable ancestor. This can be done by processing nodes in decreasing depth or using union-find? Since edges open condition depends on sum[child] > A[parent]. We can compute for each node the "jump" to highest ancestor via open edges. This is like on a tree, each node has a parent edge that may be open or closed. If open, then all self_ok positions in child can climb to parent. But for a specific node i, if self_ok true, we can follow parent pointers while open. Naively O(N^2) for a chain. Need optimize.

We can compute up[i] = highest ancestor reachable from i via open edges. Since open edges are fixed. This is a standard tree "disjoint set union" or binary lifting. N 5e5, O(N log N) fine. But we can do even simpler: Process nodes in decreasing depth? We need for each node i, if self_ok true, ans[i] = sum[find(i)] where find follows open edges. We can use DSU union of child with parent if open. Since open edges go upward, we can initialize each node as its own component. For each node i (except root), if sum[i] > A[parent[i]], then union i with parent[i] (the component representative should be the highest node). Then for a node i with self_ok true, ans[i] = sum[find(i)]? But careful: If self_ok false, we should not allow it to climb, even if some edge below? self_ok false means the node itself cannot absorb its subtree, so it shouldn't be in any component with ancestors. But DSU union of open edges would connect it to parent if sum[i] > A[parent], even though self_ok false. That would incorrectly allow ans[i] to climb. However, for positions inside the subtree of i that can absorb i's subtree (C(i)), they might climb if edge open. But for node i itself, if self_ok false, it cannot. So we need distinguish: DSU should represent "can absorb the subtree of the representative" for nodes that are self_ok? Let's think.

We want for each node i:
- If self_ok[i] true, it can absorb subtree i, so it can use open edges from i upward.
- If self_ok[i] false, it cannot absorb subtree i, so it cannot use the edge from i to parent (because to climb to parent, it would need to absorb subtree i first). However, other nodes in the subtree of i that are in C(i) (can absorb subtree i) might use the edge i->parent if open. But if C(i) is empty (self_ok false and no children propagate), then no one uses that edge. If C(i) nonempty, then edge can be used by those nodes. The DSU union should be based on whether C(i) is nonempty and sum[i] > A[parent], not just sum[i] > A[parent]. C(i) nonempty iff self_ok[i] true OR (some child c has C(c) nonempty and sum[c] > A[i]). But if self_ok[i] false and no child propagates, C(i) empty. In that case, edge i->parent should be closed for all nodes. So we need compute "active" nodes: a node i is "good" if C(i) nonempty. Actually for propagation of positions, a position K can climb from node v to parent p only if K ∈ C(v). If C(v) is empty, no position can climb through v. Thus the open edge from v to p should be considered only if C(v) is nonempty AND sum[v] > A[p]. If C(v) is empty, the edge is closed.

But for a position K that is exactly node v and self_ok[v] false, K ∉ C(v), so it won't climb. For a position K in a child of v that can absorb child and then v, it would be in C(v) if propagation condition holds. So C(v) nonempty is the relevant condition for the edge v->parent.

How to compute C(v) nonempty? Recurrence:
C(v) nonempty iff self_ok[v] OR (exists child c with C(c) nonempty and sum[c] > A[v]).
This is a bottom-up DP. Let active[v] = (C(v) nonempty).
Base leaf: self_ok true -> active true.
For internal: active[v] = self_ok[v] or (active[left] and sum[left] > A[v]) or (active[right] and sum[right] > A[v]).
Because if a child's C is nonempty and sum[child] > A[v], then those positions propagate to v, so C(v) nonempty.

Then the open edge from v to parent p is open if active[v] and sum[v] > A[p]. Because if C(v) nonempty, then all positions in C(v) can absorb subtree v, and if sum[v] > A[p], they can climb to p. If C(v) empty, no positions to climb.

For a specific node i, if self_ok[i] true, then i ∈ C(i). It can climb along edges that are open from i upward. If self_ok[i] false, i ∉ C(i), so even if active[i] true due to children, i itself cannot climb. But positions in children can climb. Thus for ans[i], we need:
- If self_ok[i] false: ans[i] = A[i].
- If self_ok[i] true: ans[i] = sum[highest ancestor reachable from i via open edges (active[v] and sum[v] > A[parent[v]])].

But wait, if self_ok[i] true, i ∈ C(i). To climb from i to parent p, we need active[i] (true) and sum[i] > A[p]. This is exactly open edge. After climbing to p, i ∈ C(p). To climb further from p to grandparent, we need active[p] (true) and sum[p] > A[grandparent]. Since i ∈ C(p), active[p] true. So open edges along the path are exactly active[v] and sum[v] > A[parent[v]]. Thus ans[i] = sum[find(i)] where find follows open edges, but only for self_ok true nodes. For self_ok false nodes, ans[i]=A[i], regardless of open edges.

Can we compute find for all nodes using DSU union of open edges? If we union v with parent[v] when active[v] and sum[v] > A[parent[v]], then for any self_ok true node i, find(i) gives highest reachable ancestor. For self_ok false node i, we don't use find. But if we union all open edges, a self_ok false node might be in a component with ancestors due to active[v] true from children, but we ignore its ans. However, could a self_ok false node be an intermediate on the path for a self_ok true descendant? Yes, if active[v] true and edge open, then descendants in C(v) can climb through v. DSU union handles that. The self_ok false node v itself is not in C(v), but DSU component includes v as a representative? Wait if active[v] true due to children, C(v) contains positions from children, not v. The component for those positions should include v and ancestors, because to climb from child to v, they absorb v. The DSU union of v with parent if open, and child with v if open, will connect them. The representative should be the highest ancestor. For a self_ok false node v, if active[v] true, it is connected to parent if open. But v itself is not a starting position that can climb; however, it can serve as an intermediate. DSU doesn't need to know self_ok for intermediate, only active. So union condition active[v] and sum[v] > A[parent[v]] is correct.

Now, how to compute active[v] bottom-up? We need process nodes in postorder (children before parent). We already compute sum and active in reversed order.
