Let's prove ans for self_ok true node i = sum[find(i)] with DSU union all edges where sum[child] > A[parent]. Because find(i) follows parent pointers only through edges satisfying sum[current] > A[parent]. Since DSU unions exactly those edges, the component representative is the highest ancestor connected via such edges. Since the tree is rooted, unioning child to parent when condition holds, the representative can be the root of the component (highest). We need implement DSU union carefully to ensure representative is the highest ancestor, not arbitrary. If we union child and parent, we can set parent of child's DSU to parent's DSU (or union by size with representative as the higher node). Since edges go upward, we can simply do dsu_parent[find(child)] = find(parent) (if condition holds), because parent is higher. But if parent already unioned to its parent, find(parent) returns the highest. This works if we process unions in any order? If we union child to parent, and later parent to grandparent, find(child) will follow to grandparent if we use path compression. We can just iterate all nodes and if parent exists and sum[i] > A[parent[i]], union(i, parent[i]) with union by setting dsu[find(i)] = find(parent[i]) (attach child component to parent component). Since the tree is acyclic and parent is ancestor, this maintains representative as highest. Need be careful with union by size: if we attach parent to child, representative would be lower, bad. So we must attach child's root to parent's root. We can implement:
def find(x):
    while dsu[x] != x: dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]; x=dsu[x]; return x
for i in 1..N:
    p = parent[i]
    if p and sum[i] > A[p]:
        ri = find(i); rp = find(p)
        if ri != rp:
            dsu[ri] = rp  # attach lower to higher
Because rp is an ancestor of ri (or same), this is safe. No need union by size; attaching child root to parent root preserves highest representative. Path compression handles.

But wait: If we process i in arbitrary order, and parent p not yet unioned to grandparent, find(p) returns p. Later when p unions to grandparent, dsu[p] updated; find(i) will follow. Good.

Now, self_ok computation:
We need know left/right children. In the Cartesian tree, if left[i] exists, then i-1 is in left subtree; if right[i] exists, i+1 is in right subtree. Is this always true with our stack algorithm? Let's prove: In a Cartesian tree with inorder, the left subtree of i contains exactly indices [L_i, i-1]. If nonempty, i-1 is the rightmost. Yes. Similarly right subtree [i+1, R_i]. So self_ok[i] = (left[i]==0 and right[i]==0) or (left[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]) or (right[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]).
But if left[i] exists, is it guaranteed that i-1 is in left subtree, not somewhere else? Yes, because the subtree interval is contiguous and contains i; all indices less than i in the subtree are in left subtree. Since the subtree interval is [L_i, R_i] and L_i <= i-1 if left child exists. The rightmost of left subtree is i-1. Good.

We can compute self_ok after building tree:
self_ok = [False]*(N+1)
for i in 1..N:
    if left[i]==0 and right[i]==0:
        self_ok[i]=True
    else:
        if left[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]: self_ok[i]=True
        if right[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]: self_ok[i]=True

Is it possible for a node with children but both immediate neighbors equal A[i] to still absorb one child by absorbing a deeper element? No, because the immediate neighbor is the boundary; to access deeper, must absorb boundary first. If boundary equal, cannot. So self_ok false.

Now, sum computation:
We can compute sum via postorder. Need an order list. We can do iterative DFS from root:
order = []
stack = [root]
while stack:
    v = stack.pop()
    order.append(v)
    if left[v]: stack.append(left[v])
    if right[v]: stack.append(right[v])
Then sum = [0]*(N+1)
for v in reversed(order):
    s = A[v]
    if left[v]: s += sum[left[v]]
    if right[v]: s += sum[right[v]]
    sum[v] = s
This works because children appear after parent in order (preorder), so reversed order has children before parent.

Alternatively, we can compute sum during Cartesian tree construction? Not needed.

Need ensure root is correct. After building, root = stack[0]? Let's check standard algorithm. At the end, stack contains the right spine from root to rightmost. The bottom stack[0] is root. parent[root]=0. So root = stack[0].

Starting pos1=5, absorb pos2=1 sum6, pos3=4<6 sum10, all. Good.

Now, what about a node with self_ok true but sum[i] > A[parent], so it climbs to parent. The answer becomes sum[parent]. Does it need to absorb the sibling subtree? We argued yes automatically because after absorbing parent, sum = sum[child] + A[parent] > A[parent] >= max(sibling). But wait, sum[parent] = sum[child] + A[parent] + sum[sibling]. The current sum after absorbing parent is sum[child] + A[parent]. This is less than sum[parent] (missing sibling). Is it guaranteed > max(sibling)? Since max(sibling) <= A[parent], and sum[child] + A[parent] > A[parent] (because sum[child] > 0), so yes > max(sibling). Thus sibling can be fully absorbed. After absorbing sibling, sum becomes sum[parent]. Good.

If parent has no sibling, sum[parent] = sum[child] + A[parent], current sum after absorbing parent equals sum[parent]. Good.

Now, we need prove the recurrence for C(u) and self_ok:
- If u has no children, C(u)={u}, self_ok true.
- If u has at least one child boundary < A_u, then starting at u can absorb that boundary element, sum becomes > A_u, and since all other elements in subtree <= A_u < new sum, u can absorb the entire subtree. Thus self_ok true. If no child boundary < A_u, then all existing immediate neighbors in the subtree equal A_u (cannot be greater), so starting at u cannot absorb anything, self_ok false.
- For a position K in a child c of u, to be in C(u), it must first be in C(c) (absorb entire child c), then absorb u. The condition to absorb u is sum[c] > A_u. If true, after absorbing u, the other child subtree (sibling) is absorbable automatically because its max <= A_u < sum[c]+A_u. Thus K ∈ C(u). Conversely, if K ∈ C(u), it must absorb c first, so K ∈ C(c), and then u, so sum[c] > A_u. This justifies propagation.

For a position K that is the node u itself, it is in C(u) iff self_ok[u]. If self_ok false, it cannot absorb its subtree, so final interval is just u (since no boundary in subtree absorbable). If self_ok true, it can absorb subtree u, then can climb to ancestors according to the same edge condition: from current node v (which contains K in C(v)), if sum[v] > A[parent], then K ∈ C(parent); otherwise stop. Thus ans[u] = sum[highest reachable ancestor] if self_ok true, else A[u].

This is a complete proof.

Now, implementation details:

Input N, list A 1-indexed.

Build Cartesian tree:
```python
left = [0]*(N+1)
right = [0]*(N+1)
parent = [0]*(N+1)
stack = []
for i in range(1, N+1):
    last = 0
    while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:
        last = stack.pop()
    if stack:
        parent[i] = stack[-1]
        right[stack[-1]] = i
    if last:
        parent[last] = i
        left[i] = last
    stack.append(i)
root = stack[0]
```
Need be careful: When setting right[stack[-1]] = i, if stack[-1] previously had a right child that was popped, that child is `last` (or the last popped among multiple). The standard algorithm sets right[stack[-1]] = i after popping, and sets left[i] = last. If multiple popped, `last` is the last popped, which is the root of the popped subtree. Is that correct? Let's test with multiple pops. Standard algorithm:
```
last = 0
while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:
    last = stack.pop()
if stack:
    right[stack[-1]] = i
if last:
    left[i] = last
parent[i] = stack[-1] if stack else 0
parent[last] = i
```
Yes, `last` is the last popped (the highest among popped? Actually the last popped is the top of stack before the final pop, which is the smallest? Let's trace: stack is decreasing. We pop while < A[i]. The last popped is the one that was closest to the top before loop ended? Example stack [5,3,2], A[i]=4. Pop 2 (last=2), pop 3 (last=3), stop at5. The popped nodes 2 and3; 3 was above 2? Actually stack bottom 5, then3, then2. Pop 2, then3. Last=3. The popped subtree root should be 3, with right child 2? In the existing tree, 3 had right child 2. So last=3 is correct. left[i]=3. Good.

Need set parent[last]=i. If last=0, no left child. If stack not empty, parent[i]=stack[-1]. But what if stack empty, parent[i]=0. Also, if stack not empty, the previous right child of stack[-1] was popped and becomes left child of i; we set right[stack[-1]]=i. Good.

Now, compute order and sum:
```python
order = []
st = [root]
while st:
    v = st.pop()
    order.append(v)
    if left[v]:
        st.append(left[v])
    if right[v]:
        st.append(right[v])
subsum = [0]*(N+1)
for v in reversed(order):
    s = A[v]
    if left[v]:
        s += subsum[left[v]]
    if right[v]:
        s += subsum[right[v]]
    subsum[v] = s
```
Since tree is binary and N up to 5e5, recursion not used.

Compute self_ok:
```python
self_ok = [False]*(N+1)
for i in range(1, N+1):
    if left[i] == 0 and right[i] == 0:
        self_ok[i] = True
    else:
        if left[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]:
            self_ok[i] = True
        if right[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]:
            self_ok[i] = True
```
Need ensure A[i-1] access when left[i] exists: i>1 because left child exists means there is at least one element left, so i-1>=1. Similarly right child exists implies i+1<=N.

DSU:
```python
dsu = list(range(N+1))
def find(x):
    while dsu[x] != x:
        dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
        x = dsu[x]
    return x

for i in range(1, N+1):
    p = parent[i]
    if p and subsum[i] > A[p]:
        ri = find(i)
        rp = find(p)
        if ri != rp:
            dsu[ri] = rp
```
Is it okay to union all i, including those with self_ok false? As argued yes. But let's double-check with a potential issue: Suppose a self_ok false node v has subsum[v] > A[parent], so we union v-parent. A self_ok true descendant d in a child c cannot reach v because edge c->v not open? But if subsum[c] > A[v]? If that were true, active[v] would be true and d could reach v. If not, d not connected to v. DSU union v-parent doesn't connect d to v because d's component is separate. So safe.

However, what if d is in the subtree of v but not in C(v), yet DSU path from d to v might be connected through a chain of open edges that eventually reaches v even though C(v) empty? If C(v) empty, there is no open edge from any child to v (by recurrence). Since to reach v, the last edge into v must be from some child c with subsum[c] > A[v] and C(c) nonempty. If C(v) empty, no such edge. Thus d cannot be connected to v. So safe.

Now, compute answers:
```python
ans = [0]*(N+1)
for i in range(1, N+1):
    if self_ok[i]:
        ans[i] = subsum[find(i)]
    else:
        ans[i] = A[i]
```
Wait, if self_ok[i] true, and find(i) returns some ancestor v. Is it guaranteed that v is self_ok? Not necessarily; v could be self_ok false but active true due to children. Example? Can a self_ok false node be the highest reachable ancestor for a self_ok true descendant? Suppose v self_ok false but active true because a child c propagates to v. Then a position in c can climb to v, and if subsum[v] > A[parent], it can climb further. If subsum[v] <= A[parent], then v is the highest reachable ancestor for that position. But v self_ok false. Is that possible? Let's construct. v has two children both boundaries equal A_v, so self_ok false. But one child c has C(c) nonempty and subsum[c] > A_v, so active[v] true. Then positions in c can absorb c, then absorb v (since subsum[c] > A_v), and after absorbing v, they can absorb the other child? Wait if v self_ok false, starting at v cannot absorb either child boundary because both equal A_v. But if starting from child c, after absorbing c, sum = subsum[c] > A_v, so it can absorb v. After absorbing v, sum = subsum[c] + A_v > A_v. The other child sibling has boundary maybe equal A_v? But current sum > A_v, so it can absorb the sibling boundary (equal A_v) and then the rest. Thus positions in C(c) can absorb the entire subtree v, so C(v) nonempty. v itself cannot. So v can be an intermediate/highest ancestor for those positions. If subsum[v] <= A[parent], they stop at v, and their final interval is the subtree v, sum[v]. The answer for those positions should be sum[v], even though v self_ok false. Our DSU find for a self_ok true descendant d in c: d is connected to c (if d in C(c)), c connected to v if subsum[c] > A[v], and v connected to parent if subsum[v] > A[parent]. If stop at v, find(d)=v, ans[d]=subsum[v]. Good. So find(i) may return a self_ok false node; that's fine. For the node v itself, self_ok false, ans[v]=A[v] (it cannot absorb its subtree), even though find(v) might be v or higher due to union v-parent. We ignore find for self_ok false. Good.

Let's test this scenario with actual numbers. Need v self_ok false but active via child. A=[2,2,1,2]? Let's build max-Cartesian leftmost max.

Therefore, with leftmost tie-breaking, a node's left child boundary is always strictly < A[i]! Because if A[i-1] = A[i], i-1 would be an ancestor, not a child. Similarly, can a node have right child boundary equal? If A[i+1] = A[i], then i+1 is to the right; when processing i+1, A[i] = A[i+1], not popped, so i+1 becomes right child of i. Thus the right child boundary is i+1 and equals A[i]. So with leftmost tie, a node can have a right child with boundary equal, but a left child boundary is strictly less. Therefore self_ok false only if the node has no left child and its right child boundary equals A[i], or no children? Wait if it has a left child, left boundary < A[i], so self_ok true. If it has no left child but a right child with boundary equal, self_ok false. If it has both children, left boundary < A[i], so self_ok true. Thus self_ok false nodes are exactly nodes with no left child and a right child whose immediate value equals A[i] (i.e., A[i+1] == A[i])? What about a node with no right child but left child boundary equal? With leftmost tie, left boundary cannot be equal as argued. So no. This simplifies further! But we don't need rely; our general self_ok condition works.

Now, we should ensure the Cartesian tree with leftmost tie gives the property that if a node has a left child, A[i-1] < A[i]. This means self_ok true for any node with left child. Is that always? Let's prove: In leftmost max Cartesian tree, parent is nearest greater to left/right with tie to left. If i has a left child, then the left subtree is nonempty, so i-1 is in it. Since i is the maximum of its subtree, A[i-1] <= A[i]. If A[i-1] == A[i], then i-1 is equal and to the left. In the Cartesian tree, the nearest greater/equal to the left of i would be i-1, so i would be a right child of i-1, not have i-1 as descendant. Thus A[i-1] < A[i]. So yes. Therefore self_ok[i] is true iff left[i] != 0 or (right[i] != 0 and A[i+1] < A[i]) or no children? Wait if left child exists, true. If no left child but right child exists, self_ok true iff A[i+1] < A[i]. If no children, true. If both children, left exists -> true. So self_ok false only for nodes with no left child and (right child exists and A[i+1] == A[i])? What about no left child and no right child -> true. No left child and right child with A[i+1] < A[i] -> true. No left child and right child with A[i+1] == A[i] -> false. This matches the chain of equal values. We can compute general condition anyway.

Now, complexity O(N α(N)) after O(N) tree build. N=5e5, Python should be fine. Need use iterative DFS to avoid recursion limit. Use sys.stdin.buffer.

Now, consider a case where a node self_ok true but its sum <= parent, and the other boundary is array end. We tested.

Now, let's think about the proof of final interval = highest reachable ancestor via open edges for self_ok true nodes. We should ensure that if a node i self_ok true and find(i)=v, then i can indeed absorb the entire subtree v. We proved by induction along open edges. If edge from current node c to parent p is open (sum[c] > A[p]), and i ∈ C(c), then i ∈ C(p). Since i ∈ C(i) (self_ok), by induction i ∈ C(v). Thus final interval at least subtree v. Since v's parent (if any) has A[parent] >= sum[v] (because edge v->parent not open, i.e., sum[v] <= A[parent]), and the other boundary is array end or an ancestor with value >= A[parent]? Wait if v has parent p, the other boundary of v's subtree is either array end or the parent of p? We argued it's >= A[p] >= sum[v]. Need formal: In a Cartesian tree, the subtree interval of v is bounded by parent p on one side and by the parent of p (or array boundary) on the other? Actually if v is a left child, parent p is to the right; the left boundary is the parent of p? Let's prove generally: The subtree interval I_v is a contiguous interval. Its immediate outside left (if exists) and right (if exists) are the nearest greater elements (with tie-breaking) bounding the interval. One of them is parent[v]. The other, if exists, is an ancestor of parent[v] with value >= A[parent[v]] (because the Cartesian tree is a max-heap; ancestors have >= values). Since sum[v] <= A[parent[v]] (stop condition), the other boundary value >= A[parent[v]] >= sum[v]. Thus both outside boundaries block. If v is root, no outside boundaries. Therefore final interval is exactly I_v. Good.

Now, if self_ok false, ans[i]=A[i]. Need ensure final interval is singleton. self_ok false means no child boundary < A[i]. With leftmost tie, this implies no left child, and if right child exists, A[i+1]==A[i]. Thus immediate right neighbor equal, and no left neighbor in subtree. What about outside the subtree? The parent of i (if any) is to the left (since no left child, parent must be left? Actually if no left child, i is on the left spine? Parent could be left or right? If i has no left child, its subtree interval starts at i. Parent could be to the left (if i is right child) or to the right (if i is left child? But if i is left child of parent, then parent is to the right, and i would have no left child but could have right child. Example A=[2,2,1], node2 no left child, parent1 to left? Actually node2 is right child of1, parent left. Node3 no left child, parent2 left. In a leftmost tie chain, nodes with no left child are right children, parent to left. Could a node with no left child be a left child of parent? If i is left child of p, then p is to the right, and i's interval is [L_i, p-1]. Since i has no left child, L_i=i, so i's interval is [i, p-1]. This is possible? Example A=[3,2,4]? Node2=2 has no left child? Tree: root3? Let's build: i1=3, i2=2 parent1 right1=2, i3=4 pop2, pop1, root3 left1? Actually tree 3 left1 right2? Node2 is right child of1, not left. Need a node as left child with no left child: A=[2,1,3]? Tree root3 left1? Let's build: i1=2, i2=1 parent1 right2, i3=3 pop2, pop1, root3 left1. Node1 has right child2, no left child, and is left child of3. So possible. Node1 self_ok? left none, right child pos2 boundary A2=1 < A1=2, self_ok true. So self_ok false only if right child boundary equal, which in leftmost tie means i+1 equal and i is parent of i+1, so i is to the left of i+1. Then parent of i is to the left (since i has no left child and is on left spine? Actually if i has right child equal, i is left child of some ancestor? Example A=[2,2,2] node2 has right child3 equal, parent1 left. So parent left. Thus i's immediate left neighbor is parent (or array boundary if root). If parent exists, A[parent] >= A[i] (equal or greater). Starting at i, right neighbor equal blocks, left neighbor parent >= A[i] blocks (strict if equal? If parent equal, also not absorbable because equal not <). So final singleton. If i is root with no left child and right child equal, left boundary none, right equal blocks, final singleton. Good.

Thus algorithm solid.

Now, let's consider memory and performance:
- Arrays A, left, right, parent, subsum, self_ok, dsu, ans of size N+1. N=5e5, Python ints memory ~28 bytes each, 8 arrays ~112 MB? Actually list of ints stores references (8 bytes) plus int objects. But small ints? Values up to 1e9, each int object. A list of 5e5 ints ~4 MB for references + int objects ~14 MB? Actually each int object 28 bytes, so 5e5*28=14 MB plus list 4 MB =18 MB per array. 8 arrays =144 MB, might be high but AtCoder memory often 1024 MB or 512 MB. Python memory could be okay if limit 1024 MB, but if 256 MB, need optimize. We can reduce arrays.

We need:
- A: list of ints length N+1.
- left, right, parent: lists of ints length N+1.
- subsum: list of ints length N+1.
- self_ok: can be bytearray or list of bool (bool refs) but bytearray better.
- dsu: list of ints length N+1.

Let's test Cartesian tree construction with equal values and stack algorithm. Need ensure parent of popped nodes set correctly. Let's write a small brute force for random arrays mentally? We can reason.

Standard algorithm:
```
for i:
    last = 0
    while stack and A[stack[-1]] < A[i]:
        last = stack.pop()
    if stack:
        parent[i] = stack[-1]
        right[stack[-1]] = i
    if last:
        parent[last] = i
        left[i] = last
    stack.append(i)
```
If stack not empty, we set right[stack[-1]] = i. But what if stack[-1] already had a right child that was not popped? In a valid Cartesian tree, the previous right child of stack[-1] is the last popped (or a subtree rooted at last). Since we popped all nodes with A < A[i], any previous right child of stack[-1] with value < A[i] is popped. If there was a previous right child with value >= A[i], it would still be on stack? Actually stack contains right spine; the right child of stack[-1] is the next node on stack. If it wasn't popped, then A[next] >= A[i], so stack[-1]'s right child remains, and we should not overwrite it? Wait if stack[-1]'s right child is the next stack element and wasn't popped, then setting right[stack[-1]] = i would overwrite it, which is wrong. But can this happen? Let's trace. Stack is increasing? For max Cartesian with pop while <, stack values are non-increasing? Example A=[5,4,6]. i1 stack[1]. i2 A4: top5<4 false, parent2=1, right1=2, stack[1,2]. i3 A6: pop2 (4<6), pop1 (5<6), stack empty. No overwrite. Example A=[5,6,4]. i1 stack[1]. i2 A6 pop1, root2 left1, stack[2]. i3 A4: top6<4 false, parent3=2, right2=3. Good.
What about stack[-1]'s right child not popped? Suppose stack [1,2] with A1=10, A2=8, new i A=9. Pop2 (8<9), stop at1 (10<9 false). The previous right child of1 was2, popped. Set right1=i. Good.
If new i A=7, no pop. stack[-1]=2, right2=3? But 2 had no right child yet (it's the last on stack). So setting right2=i is correct. If stack[-1] already had a right child, that right child would be the next stack element. If no pop, that right child has A >= A[i] and remains; but stack[-1] is not the last? Actually if stack[-1] has a right child, that right child is above it on the stack. So stack[-1] is not the top; the top is the rightmost node. We only set right[stack[-1]] when stack[-1] is the top after popping? Wait after popping, stack[-1] is the top of the remaining stack. In a Cartesian tree, the top of the stack is the rightmost node on the right spine. It may have a right child? The rightmost node has no right child by definition. So setting right[stack[-1]]=i is safe. Good.

Now, root = stack[0] after loop. If stack empty? N>=2, not empty.

Now, DSU find iterative with path compression:
```
def find(x):
    while dsu[x] != x:
        dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
        x = dsu[x]
    return x
```
Union loop:
```
for i in range(1, N+1):
    p = parent[i]
    if p and subsum[i] > A[p]:
        ri = find(i)
        rp = find(p)
        if ri != rp:
            dsu[ri] = rp
```
Since we attach ri to rp, and rp is an ancestor, no cycles. But what if ri == rp already (shouldn't before union unless already connected via another path; tree no cycles). Fine.

Now, compute self_ok:
Need left/right arrays. We can compute self_ok before freeing left/right.
```
self_ok = bytearray(N+1)
for i in range(1,N+1):
    if left[i] == 0 and right[i] == 0:
        self_ok[i] = 1
    else:
        if left[i] and A[i-1] < A[i]:
            self_ok[i] = 1
        if right[i] and A[i+1] < A[i]:
            self_ok[i] = 1
```
If left[i] exists, A[i-1] < A[i] always with leftmost tie, but keep general.

Now, output:
After DSU, compute ans in A:
```
out = []
for i in range(1,N+1):
    if self_ok[i]:
        A[i] = subsum[find(i)]
    # else A[i] remains original
    out.append(str(A[i]))
print(' '.join(out))
```
But if we overwrite A[i] for self_ok true, and later in the loop for another i we need A[p]? DSU already done, so no. Good.

However, in the ans loop, for self_ok false, A[i] original. For self_ok true, A[i] overwritten. We don't need original A after DSU. Good.

Potential issue: subsum[find(i)] for self_ok true node i could be overwritten? subsum remains. Good.

Now, let's think about memory freeing left/right before DSU to reduce peak. We need left/right to compute order and self_ok. After computing self_ok and subsum, we can set `left = right = None` and maybe `order = None` before DSU. But DSU needs parent, A, subsum, self_ok. We can do:

Now, let's consider if the iterative DFS order list uses stack that may grow O(N). Fine.

Let's write the final code:

```python
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = [0] + list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    left = [0]*(N+1)
    right = [0]*(N+1)
    parent = [0]*(N+1)
    stack = []
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        last = 0
        ai = A[i]
        while stack and A[stack[-1]] < ai:
            last = stack.pop()
        if stack:
            parent[i] = stack[-1]
            right[stack[-1]] = i
        if last:
            parent[last] = i
            left[i] = last
        stack.append(i)
    
    root = stack[0]
    
    # postorder
    order = []
    st = [root]
    while st:
        v = st.pop()
        order.append(v)
        lv = left[v]
        rv = right[v]
        if lv:
            st.append(lv)
        if rv:
            st.append(rv)
    
    subsum = [0]*(N+1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        s = A[v]
        lv = left[v]
        rv = right[v]
        if lv:
            s += subsum[lv]
        if rv:
            s += subsum[rv]
        subsum[v] = s
    
    self_ok = bytearray(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        li = left[i]
        ri = right[i]
        if li == 0 and ri == 0:
            self_ok[i] = 1
        else:
            if li and A[i-1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1
            if ri and A[i+1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1
    
    # free left/right/order
    left = right = order = None
    
    dsu = list(range(N+1))
    def find(x):
        while dsu[x] != x:
            dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
            x = dsu[x]
        return x
    
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        p = parent[i]
        if p and subsum[i] > A[p]:
            ri = find(i)
            rp = find(p)
            if ri != rp:
                dsu[ri] = rp
    
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        if self_ok[i]:
            A[i] = subsum[find(i)]
        # else A[i] remains original
    
    sys.stdout.write(' '.join(str(A[i]) for i in range(1, N+1)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check `left = right = order = None` after using left/right in self_ok. But we still need `left`/`right`? No. However, assigning `left = right = order = None` will make `left` and `right` None; no further use. Good.

Now, let's think about the problem statement: Takahashi is the K-th slime in the initial state. If he absorbs a slime, his size increases by the size of the absorbed slime. Now, let's consider if Takahashi can choose to stop before no actions? We want maximum size, so he should perform all possible actions. Since all actions increase size and cannot hurt, final maximum is the closure. We proved greedy smaller boundary is optimal. The algorithm computes closure.

Now, let's think about the proof that always absorbing the smaller boundary is optimal. At any state, if the smaller boundary x >= current sum S, then the larger boundary y >= x >= S, so no action possible. If x < S, absorbing x is possible. Absorbing x increases S to S+x. Could absorbing the larger boundary y instead (if y < S) ever lead to a larger final interval than absorbing x? Since both actions are possible, and absorbing x leaves y still adjacent and now y < S+x, so y can be absorbed later. Thus absorbing x first cannot prevent absorbing y. By induction, any sequence can be transformed to one that absorbs the smaller boundary first without reducing final set. Thus the greedy process is deterministic and optimal. Good.

Let's write final code carefully:

```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = [0] + data[1:]
    del data

    left = [0] * (N + 1)
    right = [0] * (N + 1)
    parent = [0] * (N + 1)
    st = []
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        last = 0
        ai = A[i]
        while st and A[st[-1]] < ai:
            last = st.pop()
        if st:
            parent[i] = st[-1]
            right[st[-1]] = i
        if last:
            parent[last] = i
            left[i] = last
        st.append(i)

    root = st[0]

    order = []
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        lv = left[v]
        rv = right[v]
        if lv:
            stack.append(lv)
        if rv:
            stack.append(rv)

    subsum = [0] * (N + 1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        s = A[v]
        lv = left[v]
        rv = right[v]
        if lv:
            s += subsum[lv]
        if rv:
            s += subsum[rv]
        subsum[v] = s

    self_ok = bytearray(N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        li = left[i]
        ri = right[i]
        if li == 0 and ri == 0:
            self_ok[i] = 1
        else:
            if li and A[i - 1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1
            if ri and A[i + 1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1

    left = right = order = None

    dsu = list(range(N + 1))

    def find(x):
        while dsu[x] != x:
            dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
            x = dsu[x]
        return x

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        p = parent[i]
        if p and subsum[i] > A[p]:
            ri = find(i)
            rp = find(p)
            if ri != rp:
                dsu[ri] = rp

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if self_ok[i]:
            A[i] = subsum[find(i)]

    sys.stdout.write(' '.join(str(A[i]) for i in range(1, N + 1)))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    N = data[0]
    A = [0] + data[1:]
    del data

    left = [0] * (N + 1)
    right = [0] * (N + 1)
    parent = [0] * (N + 1)

    st = []
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        last = 0
        ai = A[i]
        while st and A[st[-1]] < ai:
            last = st.pop()
        if st:
            parent[i] = st[-1]
            right[st[-1]] = i
        if last:
            parent[last] = i
            left[i] = last
        st.append(i)

    root = st[0]
    st = None

    order = []
    stack = [root]
    while stack:
        v = stack.pop()
        order.append(v)
        if left[v]:
            stack.append(left[v])
        if right[v]:
            stack.append(right[v])

    subsum = [0] * (N + 1)
    for v in reversed(order):
        s = A[v]
        if left[v]:
            s += subsum[left[v]]
        if right[v]:
            s += subsum[right[v]]
        subsum[v] = s

    self_ok = bytearray(N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        li = left[i]
        ri = right[i]
        if li == 0 and ri == 0:
            self_ok[i] = 1
        else:
            if li and A[i - 1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1
            if ri and A[i + 1] < A[i]:
                self_ok[i] = 1

    left = right = order = None

    dsu = list(range(N + 1))

    def find(x):
        while dsu[x] != x:
            dsu[x] = dsu[dsu[x]]
            x = dsu[x]
        return x

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        p = parent[i]
        if p and subsum[i] > A[p]:
            ri = find(i)
            rp = find(p)
            if ri != rp:
                dsu[ri] = rp

    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if self_ok[i]:
            A[i] = subsum[find(i)]

    sys.stdout.write(' '.join(map(str, A[1:])) + '\n')

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
